new Model the Annotation, Not the Noise: Semantic Annotation-Channel Prompt Learning against Noisy Labels

Authors: Zibin Geng, Yitong Bao, Jia Li, Xuefeng Jiang, Sheng Sun, Yuwei Wang, Min Liu

Abstract: Parameter-efficient prompt learning has become a cornerstone for adapting pre-trained vision-language models (VLMs), yet it remains highly vulnerable to label noise in few-shot Web and retrieval scenarios. Existing noise robust methods typically rely on hard sample selection or deterministic label correction, which discard valuable semantic ambiguity and overlook how observed annotations are actually generated. To bridge this gap, we reformulate noisy prompt learning through the perspective of data generation, proposing SACP, a Semantic-aware Annotation Channel-based Prompt learning framework. Instead of suppressing noise heuristically, SACP decouples latent clean-label prediction from instance-dependent annotation generation. By integrating frozen VLM semantic priors with lightweight visual residuals, SACP constructs a structured annotation channel optimized via generalized expectation-maximization with soft posteriors. Furthermore, a posterior-aware memory is introduced to preserve label uncertainty during test-time inference. Extensive experiments across different synthetic benchmarks and the real-world noisy Food101N dataset demonstrate that SACP achieves state-of-the-art performance against its counterparts in different label noise patterns, especially under severe asymmetric noise.

new Forehead ROI Localization under Real-World Imaging Degradations via Cross-View Pseudo-Labeling

Authors: Geetanjali Sharma, Gaurav Jaswal, Aditya Nigam, Raghavendra Ramachandra

Abstract: Forehead region-of-interest (ROI) localization is important for forehead-creases biometrics, particularly when the lower face is occluded. However, localization remains challenging under varying image quality, pose, and illumination. We propose Confidence and Consistency Guided Pseudo-Labeling (CCGP), which adapts Grounding DINO using unlabeled facial images filtered by detection confidence and geometric consistency between original and horizontally flipped views, without modifying the detector architecture or loss. We evaluate CCGP on five external datasets and 350 images spanning seven imaging conditions. CCGP achieves the highest mean intersection over union (mIoU) on four of five external datasets, improving from 0.415 to 0.580 on RMFRD compared with supervised Grounding DINO. On the quality-stratified subset, the largest gains occur under blur (0.487 to 0.719) and low contrast (0.615 to 0.710). CCGP also outperforms conventional self-training on RMFRD (0.580 versus 0.551 mIoU). Despite a slight in-domain performance gap (0.861 vs. 0.866 mIoU) and challenges with off-angle poses, CCGP improves cross-dataset localization under challenging imaging conditions.

new SCATR-ITS: a scalable transformer for large-area individual tree segmentation from LiDAR point clouds

Authors: Lassi Ruoppa, Verneri Sepp\"anen, Antero Kukko, Juha Hyypp\"a

Abstract: Individual tree segmentation (ITS) from LiDAR point clouds is one of the most widely studied segmentation tasks in forestry. Although deep learning methods define the state of the art on the majority of ITS benchmarks, their high computational complexity and long runtime currently prevent their adoption in large-scale operational workflows. In this work, we present SCATR-ITS, a scalable transformer model specifically designed for efficient ITS over large geographical areas. Based on the ForestFormer3D framework, SCATR-ITS transitions to superpoint-level processing to accelerate inference, with the superpoints constructed from learned embeddings. The architecture incorporates size-aware superpoint-level postprocessing, a dynamic query count module to handle high variation in forest structure, and a maximal input stride designed to minimize prediction redundancy. SCATR-ITS achieves accuracy on par with the state of the art on the FGI-EMIT and FOR-InstanceV2 datasets with an up to 8.5$\times$ higher inference speed. In addition, SCATR-ITS has a significantly lower GPU VRAM footprint during both training and inference, and exhibits excellent zero-shot generalization across multiple datasets. To demonstrate large-area ITS capabilities, SCATR-ITS is applied to a 142 km$^2$ region of Finnish national laser scanning data where it achieves an average throughput of 13.8 min/km$^2$, $\sim$18.6$\times$ faster than ForestFormer3D and approaching the speed of the unsupervised watershed algorithm, which it vastly outperforms in accuracy and segment quality. Overall, SCATR-ITS delivers state-of-the-art accuracy at speeds where operational-scale adoption becomes viable, paving the way toward countrywide deployment. The source code for SCATR-ITS and pretrained weights are available at https://github.com/ruoppa/scatr_its.

URLs: https://github.com/ruoppa/scatr_its.

new Decomposing images in a PRISM: A Single-Source Multi-View Framework for Image Representation

Authors: Eduardo Campos, Willam Cardoso, Diego Furtado, Solange Oliveira, Alneu Lopes

Abstract: Multi-view learning has shown that combining complementary views can improve predictive performance, yet many datasets provide a single raw source of information. How can multiple useful views be constructed from one source? We propose PRISM (Plural Representations of Images via Structural Maps), a framework that transforms an image into representations with distinct inductive biases through explicit structural maps, together with a protocol for measuring what each view contributes. We evaluate PRISM-HD, a hand-designed instantiation of PRISM in which the constructed views are summarized through representation-specific descriptors, on three artistic and four non-artistic image datasets. Analyses at a fixed feature budget show that the gains of the structural views are not explained by feature dimensionality alone. PRISM-HD by itself outperforms WND-CHARM on all seven datasets. Moreover, PRISM-HD and Fisher Vector provide complementary predictive signals: their equal-weight combination improves over the better individual representation by 5--8 percentage points on the artistic datasets. When combined with fine-tuned deep networks through a fixed parameter-free fusion rule, the PRISM-based representation improves performance on four of seven datasets and leaves it statistically unchanged on the remaining three, indicating that the constructed views provide predictive signals that are complementary to learned deep representations.

new SparseHPS: A Unified Model for Human Pose and Shape across Depth Cameras and LiDARs

Authors: Ilya Zaychuk

Abstract: Recovering 3D human pose and shape from point clouds is central to motion capture, robotics and self-driving vehicles, because depth cameras and LiDARs sense a person directly in metric 3D and work under any lighting. However, current approaches are each tailored to one sensor type: point density differs by more than ten times between devices, public datasets are labeled with incompatible body models, and LiDAR approaches need long input sequences to offset sparse scans. In this work, we present SparseHPS, a novel sensor-agnostic network that recovers SMPL-X pose, shape and global translation from a human point cloud with one set of weights, for single frames and streams alike. Specifically, SparseHPS encodes the cloud with a LitePT backbone over sparse voxels, represents every joint by a learnable query decoded with one common head, localizes the body by voting over voxels, and draws temporal context from a causal memory of tokens from the seven preceding frames. A single model is trained on ten datasets that mix rendered clouds with real depth-camera and LiDAR captures, all converted to one body model. Experiments demonstrate state-of-the-art results on the SLOPER4D and FreeMotion LiDAR benchmarks, where SparseHPS lowers the root-relative joint error by 22% and 11%, respectively, compared with the strongest prior method, which needs 32 frames. On real Azure Kinect captures of held-out HuMMan subjects, SparseHPS attains a joint error of 34.7 mm. Moreover, the current frame alone already yields the bulk of this accuracy.

new INCISE: Training-Free In-Context Segmentation of Surgical Videos

Authors: Yifan Xu, Bofang Zheng, Guangyu Meng, Nishchal Sapkota, Xianshi Ma, Xu Cao, Tianjiao Yu, Danny Z Chen

Abstract: Surgical video analysis requires video object segmentation (VOS), yet existing methods are commonly based on pretrained model fine-tuning using a large number of expert labels. In-context segmentation (ICS) offers the promise of bypassing this high cost by segmenting a target video using a few annotated references, which are the sole source of surgical knowledge for a frozen model. However, existing ICS methods tend to draw such references at random, which concentrate on a few views that dominate a surgical video and can miss other objects of interest (e.g., transient instruments and small structures). To address these challenges, we propose INCISE, a training-free ICS framework for surgical videos. INCISE selects unlabeled frames from multiple surgical videos that jointly cover the local appearances of a target video, and then segments the target video using these references. With only 1.7% of training frames selected and annotated as references and no target video frame labeled, INCISE obtains 67.2% mean mIoU and outperforms the best SOTA fine-tuned method by 9.2 points. We hope INCISE can help establish ICS as a practical alternative to fine-tuning in surgical video analysis and other domains with a costly labeling burden.

new ConRef: Auditing Reference Reliability in 2D/3D Registration Benchmarks

Authors: Jianfeng Yue, Feng Li, Tianyu Song, Julius Watrinet, Ulrich Eck, Nassir Navab

Abstract: Purpose: Accurate 2D/3D registration is crucial for image-guided orthopedic interventions, enabling the alignment of preoperative 3D anatomy with intraoperative X-ray observations for surgical navigation. Public benchmarks have facilitated the development and evaluation of increasingly accurate registration methods. However, as registration errors approach the uncertainty of benchmark reference poses, measured performance may be limited by reference inaccuracies rather than algorithmic errors alone. Methods: We propose Consensus Reference (ConRef), a multi-view framework for auditing reference poses in existing X-ray/computed tomography (CT) registration benchmarks. By jointly exploiting multiple X-ray observations, ConRef estimates an additional reference pose, quantifies its uncertainty, and identifies potential X-ray outliers. We validate the framework using independent anatomical evidence and a newly acquired X-ray/cone-beam CT (CBCT) phantom dataset with bead-based reference poses. Results: On our BeadPhant dataset, ConRef differed from independent bead-based references by 0.08-0.12 mm, while discrepancies with original references reached 2.06 mm on the Femur dataset. It also flagged 22 DeepFluoro X-rays as potential outliers. On Femur F5, reference choice changed the submillimeter success conclusions of the same registrations. Conclusion: Our findings demonstrate that reference discrepancies can influence the evaluation of high-accuracy 2D/3D registration methods. By providing an additional, independently validated reference and identifying potential inconsistencies, ConRef enables systematic auditing of existing benchmarks and supports more reliable evaluation of registration error.

new FACET: A Data-Efficient Regional Parametric Head Model

Authors: Mathieu Lamarre, Elly Akhoundi, Thibault Goyallon, Patrick Anderson, \'Etienne Danvoye

Abstract: Production face pipelines need parametric head models that are expressive, FACS-controllable, and buildable on a small, fixed pool of identities. More training identities would always help, but high-quality registered head scans are expensive to capture; when scaling the data is not an option, the remaining lever is architecture. We present FACET, a parametric head model that makes the most of a fixed identity pool through architectural locality rather than more scans, and makes three contributions, each isolated against a matched global-PCA ablation on our own topology, training set, and normalization. (1) A regional identity decomposition into 16 anatomical regions bypasses the N-1 rank ceiling of a global PCA, driving held-out neutral-fit error from a 1.95 mm floor down to 0.23 mm on the same training identities. (2) A learned FACS action-unit expression model with per-identity neural personalization not only stays FACS-controllable but beats a properly-regularized global expression PCA at every component count -- refuting the argument that FACS is too overcomplete to fit. (3) A dedicated neck/shoulder region reduces pose leakage into the pose-invariant upper face during unposing. Together, these show that architectural locality is the effective lever for a production head model under a fixed identity budget.

new Tracktail: Multi-View 3D Point Tracking without Depth Input

Authors: Katie L Rupp, Jonathan Wong, Sharmishtaa Seshamani, Ana Mafalda Vicente, Lili Karashchuk

Abstract: We introduce Tracktail, a novel architecture designed to efficiently track query points in 3D from multiple calibrated views without any depth input. Our architecture separates the task as a per-camera video encoding of features and a per-query decoder. Notably, camera cross-attention is only performed across queries in the decoder, allowing for efficient inference (7 FPS and less than 4 GB memory for 10 views and 100 query points). We further introduce a per-camera scene scale computed from camera geometry, which allows us to normalize inputs and predictions appropriately across a vast variety of dataset scales. We train on a synthetic Multi-View Kubric dataset as well as a novel Multi-View Point Odyssey benchmark dataset, which consists of synthetic renders of humans and animals across 499 frames. Tracktail achieves state-of-the-art results on the Panoptic Studio, DexYCB, and Multi-View Kubric benchmarks, outperforming methods that rely on estimated depth. Tracktail also demonstrates a strong ability to maintain accurate tracking over longer ranges compared to existing methods. Our code, data, and weights are publicly available: https://ai-hhmi.github.io/tracktail/

URLs: https://ai-hhmi.github.io/tracktail/

new CADENCE:Temporally Coherent Whole-Body Human Mesh Recovery that Preserves Expressive Hand Motion

Authors: Keniel Peart, Jagmohan Chauhan, Indu P. Bodala, Shelly Vishwakarma

Abstract: Whole-body SMPL-X estimators recover body, hand, and face meshes from single images, but applied frame by frame to video they produce jittery motion. Smoothing the output removes the jitter at a cost that body-only benchmarks do not measure: a temporal filter strong enough to steady the torso also flattens the fast finger strokes that carry meaning in sign language and gesture. We present CADENCE, a whole-body SMPL-X system for monocular video designed around this trade-off. A per-frame estimator built on a shared C-RADIOv4 encoder, with a high-resolution hand pathway that reuses the same encoder on hand crops, produces each frame's estimate. An offline temporal refiner then filters the SMPL-X rotation sequence: attention along the kinematic tree selects, for every joint and every frame, a mixture of learned zero-phase filters, so the torso is smoothed while fast finger strokes pass through. On 3DPW, the refiner reduces acceleration error from 17.4 to 5.4 mm/s2 while lowering PA-MPJPE from 41.9 to 40.2 mm; generic smoothers applied to the same predictions reach 6.2 to 7.7 mm/s2. On SignAvatars/How2Sign sign-language sequences, the refiner has the lowest hand jitter among the compared smoothers while keeping a hand-speed peak amplitude of 0.98, against 0.35 for a SmoothNet retrained on the full SMPL-X vector. The per-frame estimator also obtains the lowest full-body mesh error (25.7 mm) and hand joint error (8.3 mm) among the compared methods on EHF.

new Spatial Query Execution as Verifiable Rewards for Multi-step Spatial Reasoning

Authors: Jiyoon Pyo, Dongwon Jung, Zekun Li, Nayeon Kim, Muhao Chen, Yao-Yi Chiang

Abstract: Large vision-language models (LVLMs) remain unreliable on spatial reasoning problems that require composing grounded relations across multiple objects. Yet, scaling reasoning supervision is challenging since human-annotated visual datasets often rely on fixed relational annotations and coarse object localization, making it difficult to obtain precise intermediate reasoning states across diverse spatial configurations and complexities. We introduce FARON (Fully Automated Reasoning Over spatial relatioNs), a framework that executes spatial queries to provide both execution-grounded reasoning supervision and verifiable outcome rewards. Rather than relying on existing annotated data, FARON constructs synthetic 2D and 3D scenes with explicit geometries, enabling configurable object arrangements, spatial relations, and multi-step compositions. Deterministic query execution yields intermediate sets and final answers, providing precise supervision without manual relation annotation. Our pipeline converts intermediate states into natural-language reasoning traces for supervised fine-tuning (SFT), while the execution-derived answers provide verifiable rewards for reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR). Controlled experiments show that trace-based SFT improves multi-step spatial reasoning over final-answer supervision and that subsequent group relative policy optimization (GRPO) provides further gains. The resulting models also demonstrate transfer across external spatial reasoning benchmarks and multiple LVLM backbones without benchmark-specific fine-tuning. These results show the potential of using spatial query execution as a unified, flexible source of reasoning supervision and verifiable outcome rewards for multimodal spatial reasoning.

new 3D Open-Vocabulary Detection via Visual Alignment and Generative Captions

Authors: Thomas E. Huang, Alex Zihao Zhu, Hao Xiang, Tianyi Cai, Jingwei Ji, Yuliang Zou, Jiafan Yu, Xin Huang, Mingxing Tan

Abstract: Open-vocabulary 3D object detection remains a significant challenge for outdoor scenes, with existing methods often struggling to effectively transfer the rich semantic knowledge from powerful 2D Vision-Language Models (VLMs) into a 3D context. We propose a novel, two-part framework for this task. First, we introduce a direct, object-level distillation method. We generate class-agnostic 3D proposals to source regions from the entire scene, without relying on any manual labels. We leverage a spatially-aware VLM to extract a semantic embedding from each proposal's projected 2D bounding box and supervise our 3D model to predict this embedding on a per-instance basis. Second, to enhance zero-shot capability and inject domain-specific information, we employ a powerful Multimodal Large Language Model (MLLM) as an image captioner to generate rich textual captions for each of these proposals. These captions are integrated into the training objective to directly align the 3D features with these rich semantics, creating a composite loss that combines direct feature distillation with caption-based semantic enrichment. Our experiments validate the effectiveness of our approach, establishing a new state of the art in 3D open-vocabulary detection on the Waymo Open Dataset and nuScenes.

new Scoliosis5K: Multimodal Gait Analysis via Clip-Conditioned RGB-Silhouette Fusion

Authors: Zizhao Peng, Zihan Wang, Zheng Lv, Yan Wang, Ping Li, Fengwei An

Abstract: Color walking video records body boundary motion alongside clothing folds, surface appearance, and recording-environment cues. To examine what an explicit binary silhouette contributes to participant-level classification of scoliosis-related gait when a classifier already observes the paired color sequence, we construct Scoliosis5K, a multi-site collection of 4,998 corridor-walking sequences across 3,010 participant identifiers in which RGB person crops, binary silhouettes, and two-dimensional poses share each recording identifier and frame index. Our architecture uses each patch's pixel deviation from its clip mean to weight two low-rank query and value updates inside a pretrained RGB encoder, fusing the resulting video representation with a silhouette transformer sequence and regional kernel ridge scores extracted from one native-resolution RGB frame per window. Across 988 participants in five participant-disjoint cross-validation folds and three training seeds, the complete system reaches 83.77% accuracy and 79.91% macro-averaged F1, compared with 83.27% accuracy and 79.56% macro-F1 when the video RGB encoder remains frozen under identical inputs. Zeroing the silhouette vector at fixed weights shifts overall accuracy by only 0.10 percentage points while raising positive-category recall from 68.52% to 77.78% and lowering recall for both remaining categories. Macro-averaged AUROC is 0.950 across the cohort and 0.879 among participant pairs matched on acquisition site and recording-count group.

new FlowScope: 3D Scene Flow Pretraining Improves Surgical Robot Policies

Authors: John J. Han, Adam Schmidt, Jingpei Lu, Zooey He, Muhammad Abdullah Jamal, Jie Ying Wu, Omid Mohareri

Abstract: Imitation learning for surgical robots is limited by the cost of collecting demonstrations. Meanwhile, many robot-assisted procedures are recorded as stereo video, yet policies cannot learn from them directly; clinical video carries no action labels. We present FlowScope, which pretrains a surgical policy network on unlabeled clinical video by forecasting scene flow, the metric 3D motion of scene points, with labels computed by off-the-shelf stereo depth and optical flow models. We pretrain the trunk of an Action Chunking Transformer (ACT) on a dataset of 1,149 clinical procedures (1.35M clips) and discard the flow head afterward, so the deployed policy is a standard ACT with no added inference cost. On a real surgical robotic system, FlowScope raises needle pickup success from 54% to 90% and first-handover success on needle regrasp from 4% to 52% (full task: 2% to 14%) over training from scratch (n=50 trials each), and retains its gains with a generative flow-matching action head. Ablations indicate that metric 3D motion is the strongest pretraining signal among those tested; kinematics, 2D tracks, depth alone, and scale-free 3D flow, each paired with kinematics, yield smaller or no gains. Representation analyses suggest that scene-flow pretraining encodes metric needle depth more accurately, that kinematics pretraining increases reliance on the robot state, and that needle-depth probes follow the ordering of real-robot success where offline validation error does not.

new SonoAtlasWorld: Anatomy-Grounded Ultrasound World Models for Wide-Range Scanning

Authors: Zheyuan Zhang, Boris Mailhe, Hang Yu, Yikang Liu, Xiao Chen, Ankush Mukherjee, Terrence Chen, Shanhui Sun

Abstract: Learning robotic ultrasound policies requires interactive environments for scalable data generation and repeatable evaluation, while exploration on real patients is limited by acquisition costs and safety constraints. Generative world models offer an alternative, but image history and probe actions alone do not ensure anatomical consistency across wide-range scans and changing acoustic windows. We introduce SonoAtlasWorld, an ultrasound world model built on anatomy as state. A persistent 3D anatomical asset extracted from computed tomography (CT) anchors each scan to a specific anatomy. Probe actions determine anatomical and acoustic visibility conditions for a causal ultrasound generator trained on clinical videos. Our three-stage pipeline combines anatomy-conditioned pretraining, causal conversion and long-rollout alignment. Inference optimizations achieve pose-to-ultrasound throughput of 81.2 FPS. To evaluate anatomical control beyond image similarity, we introduce the Action-Following Index (AFI), which measures normalized reconstruction gain over a static-prefix baseline. On 40 held-out 3D volumes, SonoAtlasWorld achieves liver and macro-organ AFI of 0.428 and 0.262, compared with at most 0.117 and 0.038 for the tested action- and geometry-conditioned methods. Long-rollout alignment reduces brightness drift by 3.46x at the end of 40 repeated scan cycles. SonoAtlasWorld also generates offline policy-training demonstrations and supports anatomically grounded closed-loop evaluation. Scaling the generated training data improves key-plane acquisition success from 48.2% to 81.2% on unseen anatomies in simulation, demonstrating its utility for robotic ultrasound policy learning.

new Effectiveness of Synthetic Defects for Anomaly Detection in Industrial Small Load Carriers

Authors: Vincent Wahyudi, Kuniko Paxton, Amila Akagi\'c, Martin Storath, Dhavalkumar Thakker

Abstract: Recent industrial anomaly detection methods use procedural noise to generate synthetic training defects. Their effectiveness in real-world settings and the impact of different noise generation methods have not been sufficiently investigated. We evaluate the effectiveness of training using synthetic defects for both supervised and unsupervised methods, the impact of different noise generation methods, and the differences in detection performance between benchmark datasets and a real-world small load carrier (SLC) dataset. Trained on synthetic defects only, a supervised classifier reaches 0.65 AUROC on real SLC defects, while three unsupervised methods using the same kind of noise (Perlin) reach 0.87--0.90. Except for SuperSimpleNet, which fails with the foreground-confined fractal Brownian motion (fBm) recipe, models trained with the custom recipe or with nine other noise types do not differ reliably from those trained with Perlin noise. Finally, results with synthetic defects on benchmark datasets do not carry over to the SLC dataset, possibly because the synthetic defects differ too much from the real ones (a larger angle in feature space). These results call for caution when training models on synthetic defect images.

new TouchBench: Can Vision-Language Models Feel Invisible Physical Properties?

Authors: Ziyao Zeng, Ziyi Kou, Chuan Qin, Mia Huang, Roger Boldu, Zhiwen Fan, Daniel Rakita, Eric Whitmire, Li Guan

Abstract: Vision-language models (VLMs) are increasingly used for embodied perception and reasoning, yet their physical understanding is largely evaluated on questions answerable from images. Many properties critical to manipulation, such as an opaque bottle's fill level or an object's compliance, may only be revealed through touch. We introduce TouchBench, an egocentric vision-language-tactile benchmark that distinguishes visually answerable questions (Tier A) from those involving properties hidden from the camera (Tier B). It pairs head-mounted video with synchronized 137-channel whole-hand tactile sensing, comprising 900 clips from five participants across 11 tasks, with 900 absolute and 4,102 relative questions. Four state-of-the-art VLMs average 66.4% accuracy on Tier A relative questions but only 49.8% on Tier B, near chance (50%). To bridge this gap, we propose Tactile-as-Prompt (TaP), which presents 34 interpretable tactile statistics as text, enabling VLMs to reason with touch without a learned tactile encoder. TaP raises zero-shot Tier B relative accuracy to 62.9% and reaches 73.1% after leave-one-user-out fine-tuning, outperforming video alone (67.4%) and a learned tactile-token encoder (67.3%). The gains extend to unseen objects and users. On a separate 20-hour in-the-wild corpus, tactile text improves fine-tuned contact-question accuracy from 67.1% to 75.5%. These results highlight the limits of vision-only physical reasoning and establish language as an effective interface for tactile perception.

new Perceive Once, Escalate on Demand: Handing Off Visual KV Memory from Small to Large VLMs

Authors: Qiang Zhou, Jianing Deng, Siyan Dong, Jingxian Chen, Han-Yi Lin, Jingtong Hu

Abstract: An embodied agent cannot afford a large vision-language model (VLM) at every step of a long video, so a cascade runs a small VLM continuously and escalates only uncertain decisions to a large one. Each escalation, however, forces the large model to re-encode a history the small model has already perceived, and this dominates its cost. We present Baton, which hands the large model the small model's visual memory, its key-value (KV) cache over the history. Baton rests on one finding: the large model needs a usable memory, not a faithful copy of its own. A map trained to reproduce the large model's cache matches it most closely, yet brings no gain over the small model alone. Baton instead distills a per-layer linear map, without labels, from the large model's own answers, with the large model encoding only the current frame. Interventions on the map trace the gain to one direction, carried by the middle third of the large model's layers. On RoboMME, Baton beats the small model alone, a pruned or shortened re-watch, and a text memory; as the escalation channel of a cascade it matches re-watching to within about one point at 28-47% less compute, and 8x less per escalation at 64 frames. Its gain grows with the large model, from 8B to 32B, tracks the large model's headroom across eight model pairs from four families, and transfers zero-shot to OpenEQA.

new What Does a Zoom Actually Add? Decomposing Fine-Grained Perception Failures in Vision-Language Models

Authors: Zhixuan Ge, Anqi Li, Kexin Geng, Wei Qiu

Abstract: Zooming into a region often turns a wrong answer from a vision-language model (VLM) into a right one, and a growing literature takes this as evidence that the model lacked the pixels. But a zoom changes several things at once. It removes the surrounding context, re-tokenizes, at a higher density, pixels the model has already seen, and, if the first pass was downsampled, exposes detail the model never received. We separate these factors for each failure. Holding the first pass fixed, we vary only the second look and assign every failure to the cheapest one that fixes it: reusing cached tokens, isolating the region, re-tokenizing the seen pixels, or acquiring new ones. The study covers 2,753 zoom-rescued failures of Qwen2.5-VL, Qwen3-VL and InternVL3 on V*, ZoomBench and TextVQA, at first-pass budgets from 17x fewer pixels than the source to native resolution. First, most rescued failures did not lack the pixels: upsampling the pixels the model had already seen fixes 47-73% of them even at 17x downsampling, against 1-3% for a random region. Second, the first-pass budget decides how often new pixels are needed: depending on the benchmark, new pixels are required for 23-50% of the rescues at 17x and for 12-23% at 4x. The missing-pixel reading and the accounts that dispute it thus hold at different budgets. Third, the useful magnification is large and differs between objects and text: seen pixels fix more failures up to 6-12x their first-pass size per side on objects and saturate by 4x on text, and even native pixels gain from being rendered beyond native resolution. Allowing any magnification, 68-94% of the failures at 17x need no new pixels. These models more often need a better rendering of evidence they already have than new pixels, which motivates re-tokenizing a region before acquiring it.

new What Does the Visual Encoder Buy? Visual Information Gain in Encoder-Free Multimodal Pretraining

Authors: Zhixuan Ge, Anqi Li, Wei Qiu

Abstract: Recent scaling studies ask whether vision-language models still need a visual encoder by comparing encoder-free and encoder-based models on the next-token loss of the text in image-text data. This loss can fall without any use of the image, and two runs with the same loss can differ in what they take from it. Visual information gain (VIG) is the loss increase when the image is replaced by a uniform one. It splits exactly into global information, carried by image statistics, and layout information, which shuffling the patches within the image destroys. We apply it to decoders with 158M to 1.04B parameters that read pixel patches through a linear embedding, evaluated every billion tokens up to 40B, and to 19 public checkpoints. In our decoders the image lowers the loss by 3% to 46%, depending on the image type. Global information is present after the first billion tokens. Layout information starts near zero, grows slowly with training and does not grow with model size on five of six image types, and a larger batch reaches the same loss with less of it. With a frozen SigLIP 2 encoder in front of the same decoder the loss is lower only when the arrangement of the image is intact. On shuffled images the losses with and without the encoder agree to within 0.04 nats on five of six image types, so the net advantage of the encoder in the loss is layout information, above all the ability to read text in images. Public encoder-free models at production scale (Gemma 4 12B, NEO, Mono-InternVL) do acquire layout information. On the same documents they have 0.53 to 0.76 nats of it, against 0.65 to 0.82 for recent encoder-based models and 0.04 to 0.07 for our decoders, and their released training stages show it rising. These results agree with the reported parity of the two families when both are trained from scratch and place the reported advantage of a pretrained encoder in layout information.

new Tokenize Moments, Not Frames: Tokenizer-Aware Budget Allocation for Training-Free Video LLM Acceleration

Authors: Zirui Zhao, Kai Liu, Linghe Kong

Abstract: Video large language models (LLMs) represent each video with thousands of visual tokens, and training-free accelerators that prune or merge these tokens lose accuracy on long videos. Analyzing frame similarity and the vision tokenizers of 16 open models, we trace this loss to a step before token selection. Under a token budget, the frames of a long video are sampled farther apart than the roughly 2 s over which frames remain similar. The two-frame 3D patches of models such as Qwen-VL then blend two unrelated scenes into each visual token, a double exposure that arises before any pruning or merging. Fixed-size tokenizers such as InternVL's avoid this blend but respond differently to the same reduction. We propose ESSENCE, a training-free method that tokenizes moments, segments within which frames remain similar, rather than frames. Before encoding, a closed-form rule uses the sampling interval and the tokenizer class to set the number of token grids and the dimension to reduce; visual tokens are then released from later LLM layers. Against five recent accelerators on Qwen3-VL, Qwen2.5-VL and InternVL3.5, ESSENCE achieves higher accuracy in 58 of 60 comparisons at matched mean time to first token (TTFT). With 80% of the tokens, it matches the average accuracy of the full Qwen models at $1.6\times$ faster TTFT; on GLM-4.1V, it exceeds the full model by up to 3.4 points. Code is available at: https://github.com/zhkandsrz/ESSENCE.

URLs: https://github.com/zhkandsrz/ESSENCE.

new SecretAgent: A Multi-Agent Framework for Privacy Protection against Vision Language Models

Authors: Minda Zhao, Yue Zhang, Mengyu Wang

Abstract: Vision-language models (VLMs) can infer private information from shared images, including locations, personally identifiable information (PII), and personal attributes such as age and sex, even from medical images. Adversarial perturbations can protect such information against black-box VLMs, but existing methods overlook image quality and introduce visible artifacts into the images they protect. We present SecretAgent, the first general multi-agent framework that protects image privacy against VLMs while preserving visual fidelity. LLM agents iteratively locate the visual evidence that reveals private information and adapt an invisible perturbation to the target VLM's answers and to an appearance reviewer agent, which rejects any candidate with visible artifacts. We instantiate SecretAgent with two complementary variants: SecretAgent-Gradient optimizes the perturbation through six surrogate encoders for cross-model transferability, and the gradient-free SecretAgent-Tool composes procedural noise and achieves the best perceptual fidelity on three of the five datasets. Experiments on four privacy applications (geolocation, PII, facial, and medical) across five datasets and six VLMs show that SecretAgent preserves image quality substantially better than existing methods while achieving state-of-the-art privacy protection in every application, and that its protection transfers to other VLMs.

new Looking Right Is Not Being Right: Evaluating the Geometry of Agent-Built 3D Scenes

Authors: Ziyang Long, Yen-Liang Lin, Shifan Zhu, Allen Tu, Meher Gitika Karumuri, Chenqi Li, Srikumar Sastry, Sarosij Bose, Mellon M. Zhang, Hsin-Jung Yang

Abstract: Vision-language agents can reconstruct rooms from video as editable 3D scenes, but convincing renders do not establish geometric accuracy. We introduce a scan-referenced evaluation and use controlled experiments to study reconstruction accuracy, the role of geometric inputs, and agents' ability to verify and correct their scenes. Our geometric score measures surface, depth, and furniture placement agreement with laser scans of 30 real rooms on a 0-100 scale. The strongest tested agent, GPT-6 Astra, scores 51.4 from video alone, while a dense reconstruction from the same video scores 75.9. Given the point cloud used for that reconstruction, Astra produces an editable scene that scores within three points of it. Agents also inherit input errors: rescaling the supplied geometry changes scene scale almost proportionally. During revision, viewing renders improves appearance more consistently than geometry. Logs of their tool calls show that agents repeatedly measure their inputs but rarely compare them with the scenes they build. To provide this missing check, we introduce a geometric error report that compares each saved scene with the agent's input points and lists the largest discrepancies. Without new geometric evidence, the report raises the revision gains of GPT-6.1 Terra and Sol by 3.9 and 5.5 points over concurrent controls.

new Learning Video Foresight Reasoning from Unlabeled Videos via On-Policy Self-Distillation

Authors: Jingchen Sun, Jiaxing Zhang, Qingqiao Hu, Dingqiang Ye, Yuqi Jia, Yijun Liang, Di Fu, Yunjie Tian, Dongdi Zhao, Changyou Chen

Abstract: Video foresight reasoning requires vision-language models to anticipate future events or next actions from currently observed videos. Existing methods fit future captions, rationales, or full visual states, but face two limitations. First, they rely on annotations or external large models for supervision, limiting scalability. We address this with an on-policy self-distillation framework that turns past-future video pairs into a natural self-supervised signal without extra annotations or LLM supervision. Second, they treat the whole future clip as equally valuable, although most of it is irrelevant context information. We address this by defining a foresight score, from which we obtain a contrastive privilege advantage that drives the model to learn the most valuable future information. Since the score is intractable, we optimize a tractable lower-bound surrogate that interpolates this advantage, computed between the true and a random reference future, with a teacher-student distillation advantage; together, they amplify decision-relevant content information and suppress irrelevant context information. We call the resulting framework Future-OPSD. On open-ended prediction and closed-ended planning, Future-OPSD improves base models by up to 14.2 and 15.9 points without compromising general video understanding. Code will be available at https://jingchensun.github.io/future-opsd/

URLs: https://jingchensun.github.io/future-opsd/

new Causal Diffusion for Extreme Super-Resolution in Interactive Games

Authors: Christina Zhang, Jenna Kang, Colin Groth, Akshay Jindal, Stavros Diolatzis, Qi Sun

Abstract: Existing game super-resolution methods typically operate at moderate upscaling factors, struggling to recover fine visual details from extremely low-resolution inputs (e.g., 120p). At such resolutions, super-resolution shifts from reconstructing existing information toward synthesizing missing visual details. While video diffusion models offer powerful generative priors for this challenging task, their dependence on future frames limits their applicability to interactive games. We present CIDER, a causal diffusion framework for extreme game super-resolution that synthesizes high-frequency details from current and past low-resolution frames, guided by G-buffers (depth and motion vectors) to preserve scene structure and temporal consistency. We collect data from two AAA games (Cyberpunk 2077 and Black Myth: Wukong). To reduce inference load, we develop a lightweight latent projection encoder tailored to ultra-low-resolution inputs, fine-tune a decoder (TAE) on our high-resolution outputs, and distill the diffusion model to reach interactive frame rates. Our experiments demonstrate high-fidelity 8$\times$ upscaling from 120p to 960p at around 13 FPS, outperforming state-of-the-art game upscalers in visual quality and showing potential for 16$\times$ magnification.

new When Perception Collides with Priors: Diagnosing Modal Polarization in Video Understanding Models

Authors: Yao Tong, Jiaxin Qi, Yan Cui, Jinli Ou, Yuhua Zheng, Jianqiang Huang

Abstract: Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have achieved strong video comprehension, largely benefiting from extensive training on realistic video data. However, when video perception severely conflicts with internal priors or common sense, we identify a critical failure pattern termed Modal Polarization, where models fail to integrate modalities and instead bifurcate into two distinct extremes: Cognitive Inertia, where models rigidly adhere to priors despite contradictory visual evidence, and Perceptual Inertia, where they blindly trust video perception while ignoring logical inconsistencies. To investigate this, we introduce TheaterBench, a benchmark designed to expose Modal Polarization using counter-intuitive scenarios that violate physical laws or common sense - situations rarely encountered in standard training distributions. Extensive experiments reveal that current state-of-the-art (SOTA) models exhibit severe Modal Polarization, and many de-hallucination methods merely rebalance the trade-off between Perceptual and Cognitive Inertia rather than fundamentally resolving the conflict. This work serves as a foundational step for evaluating and enhancing the perceptual-cognitive equilibrium of future video understanding models.

new ViewDreamer: A Robot World Model beyond Fixed Camera Setups

Authors: Ye Mao, Jinhui Xiong, Egor Larionov, Shiyang Lu, Krystian Mikolajczyk, Wei Ye

Abstract: Video generation models are increasingly important for robot learning, yet most generate each camera view separately or tile all views in a fixed layout. Robot demonstrations, however, are captured by synchronized cameras whose number, placement, and order vary across embodiments. We present ViewDreamer, a single world model that handles variable sets of cameras by disentangling per-view generation from cross-view interaction. Each view follows the generation path of a pretrained single-view model, and views interact only through shared slot tokens. We stabilize multi-view training with a curriculum that gradually increases the number of views. Cross-View Slot Agreement uses visual foundation models to group geometrically and semantically similar regions across views into shared slots. On RoboView-Bench, which covers one to four cameras, ViewDreamer achieves the best generation quality among the models evaluated and improves cross-view consistency over independent per-view generation. These gains carry over to our world-action model (WAM) and action-conditioned world model (AC-WM). Our WAM outperforms the FastWAM-Joint baseline on LIBERO-Plus, RoboTwin 2.0, and RoboDojo, and on a real Franka robot. Both variants improve out-of-domain generalization to various camera settings and scenes.

new SceneRuler: Measuring Physical Quantities in Video via Minimal Sufficient Reconstruction

Authors: Yumeng He, Gezheng Kang, Fanlian Zeng, Yin Yang, Chenfanfu Jiang

Abstract: Many questions about videos ask for a number, such as how tall a person is, how far a cart travels, or how fast a ball leaves a ramp. We study this task, quantitative physical reasoning from video, where the inputs are a video, a question, and a prior of known facts such as object sizes, speeds, and distances from the camera to named objects. End-to-end video-language models lack an explicit metric scale and physical consistency, and their errors cannot be inspected. We present SceneRuler, which instead recovers a measurable physical scene and answers with that scene as evidence. Its central principle, Minimal Sufficient Reconstruction, builds, for each question, only the smallest scene that suffices to measure the asked quantity, and tests sufficiency on measurements rather than assuming it. The scene is written as a program whose structure a vision-language model writes and whose magnitudes are left to measurement. Physics-guided Fitting measures them through physical motion laws, so that gravity itself can anchor the metric scale, and marks every parameter, and the read-out of the asked quantity, as determined or undetermined. Each question receives a read-out and an answer. The read-out is the asked quantity evaluated on the scene, and it is empty when the scene does not fix that quantity. The answer is always given by a model that takes the scene as evidence. We evaluate on QuantiPhy and also apply the method to 27 in-the-wild videos that we collected, which have no ground-truth answers. On its validation split, the final answer reaches a mean relative accuracy of 0.709 against 0.624 for the strongest baseline, and the reconstruction determines the asked quantity on 47 of 158 questions, where the final answer reaches 0.781. Because every intermediate quantity is explicit, each answer can be traced to the objects, scale, and motion behind it.

new S2D-Splat: Static-First Gaussian Reconstruction of Dynamic Driving Scenes with Layer-Wise Feature Guidance

Authors: Jinheng Li, Tianchen Deng, Bufan Zhang, Qu Chen, Jianwu Fang

Abstract: Moving objects introduce cross-frame inconsistencies that complicate camera estimation and static geometry recovery in driving scenes. Recent feed-forward Gaussian methods explicitly separate static and dynamic primitives, but often estimate camera parameters and geometry from shared features that jointly encode both components, leaving static reconstruction susceptible to dynamic interference. To address this challenge, we propose S2D-Splat, a feed-forward framework for static-first Gaussian reconstruction with layer-wise feature guidance. Our framework separates static and dynamic features before camera and geometry estimation, enabling the static branch to recover camera parameters and static 3D Gaussians with reduced dynamic interference. Intermediate static features are cached at each layer and reused as fixed conditioning at corresponding layers of the dynamic branch. This asymmetric information flow preserves the established static representations while providing stable geometric context for reconstructing dynamic 3D Gaussians. An instance-level motion decoder further queries the fused static and dynamic representations to estimate object trajectories, supporting motion-aligned Gaussian fusion and temporal interpolation. Experiments on Waymo and zero-shot evaluations on nuScenes and the MM-AU accident dataset demonstrate that S2D-Splat outperforms existing approaches on most evaluated metrics and generalizes to highly dynamic, pre-collision driving scenarios.

new Real-Time On-Drone Hyperspectral Anomaly Detection for Wilderness Search and Rescue

Authors: Jacqueline Liu (Kic'i), Gyabaah Kyere (Kic'i), Chengyan Liu (Kic'i), Kenneth Chiu (Kic'i), Lynn Marie (Kic'i), Church, Sean Gleason, Jayson Boubin

Abstract: Wilderness search-and-rescue (WSAR) teams use uncrewed aerial systems (UASs) to survey terrain for indicators of missing persons that are rare, blend into the background, or lack thermal signatures. Hyperspectral imaging (HSI) provides rich spectral information, but its high data volume challenges real-time on-drone processing. We present SABL, an edge-computing pipeline for on-drone Spectral Anomaly-Based Localization. SABL integrates hyperspectral streaming with exponentially moving Reed-Xiaoli (ERX) anomaly detection and DBSCAN spatial filtering to group pixel-level anomalies into candidate search regions. SABL is evaluated on data collected during a 10-day deployment with Quinhagak Search and Rescue (QSAR) in remote Alaska. It processes hyperspectral scanlines at 5.67-5.73 ms per line, meeting the camera's 10-ms acquisition interval; DBSCAN requires only 6.5-12.5 ms per 100-line update for filtering. SABL achieves approximately 56% and 99% object-level precision on QSAR-selected search indicators across two scenes, with corresponding recall of 55% and 29%, providing significant background filtering while identifying difficult-to-see anomalous search indicators.

new ReactDrive: Self-Reactive Driving World Model

Authors: Yifan Wang, Xiaoqi Wang, Cho-Ying Wu, Wenbin He, Chenyu You, Liu Ren

Abstract: The world evolves through the dynamics of its constituent entities and their interactions, with changes in one entity influencing the behavior of others. To simulate this evolution under different actions, interactive world models need to capture the resulting agent responses as part of the world's own dynamics. In autonomous driving, errors in modeling these responses can misrepresent maneuver safety and undermine the reliability of generated videos used for policy evaluation and learning. To formalize this intrinsic requirement for driving world model, we define self-reactivity as the capability to model agent responses from observed history and a specified ego action, and generate visual futures that reflect those responses without prescribed future agent trajectories. To realize this capability, we introduce ReactDrive, a driving world model that jointly learns agent dynamics and visual generation through a shared agent state representation. Within this framework, dynamics supervision grounds a reactive dynamics prior in observed agent states, while the video objective jointly optimizes the self-reactivity and visual realism. Experiments show improved agent responses, ego controllability, and video quality, a 96.0% preference for agent reactivity in human comparisons across five baselines, and better downstream planning through augmentation with generated videos. We hope this work encourages the community to recognize self-reactivityas a foundational capability for developing and evaluating interactive world models. Project page: https://reactdrive.github.io/ .

URLs: https://reactdrive.github.io/

new TSIR: Ray-Traced Triangle Splats for Inverse Rendering

Authors: Wentao Yang, Chentao Shen, Zhengqi Zhang, Bolin Chen, Yan Ye, Xiangru Huang

Abstract: Inverse rendering enables realistic relighting for visual effects and augmented reality. However, overlapping Gaussian primitives complicate surface recovery and require costly multi-layer traversal and compositing for light transport. We propose TSIR, a two-stage inverse rendering method that reconstructs relightable triangle splats from multi-view images. The first stage prepares opaque triangle splats with learnable vertex shading normals. The second stage directly traces these surfaces to estimate materials through Monte Carlo integration of direct illumination and indirect reflections, reducing reliance on multi-layer compositing. Anti-aliasing improves rendering quality while maintaining efficiency. Experiments demonstrate higher relighting quality than the evaluated baselines and more efficient anti-aliasing than supersampling.

new Towards Text-Mask Consistency in Medical Image Segmentation

Authors: Jie Gui, Hang Tu, Wen Sha, Xiuquan Du

Abstract: Vision-language models for medical image segmentation often produce masks that conflict with the accompanying text, especially under multi-site/multi-lesion descriptions. We trace this failure to two factors: (i) highly templated and repetitive clinical language causes one-to-one hard contrastive learning to yield numerous false negatives, weakening cross-modal alignment; and (ii) predominantly vision-driven, one-way cross-attention lacks a language-dominant, spatially aware pathway, hindering effective injection of textual semantics into the spatial visual domain. To this end, we propose Consistency-enhanced Two-stage Segmentation (C2Seg). In the pretraining stage, Cluster-aware Contrastive Learning uses a frozen strong baseline to construct an intra-batch text similarity matrix as soft labels, thereby alleviating false negative conflicts and producing more discriminative visual representations. In the fusion stage, we introduce a Bidirectional Complementary Attention Module, where each modality dominates attention along its own path, fostering deep interaction and structural consistency between visual and textual representations. In order to enhance the expressive power of multimodal features, we further adopt KAN-based Attention Gating. Without updating the language encoder, our approach significantly improves text-mask consistency and segmentation accuracy on four public medical imaging datasets. The source code is available at https://github.com/JGuiidev/C2Seg.

URLs: https://github.com/JGuiidev/C2Seg.

new Learning 3D MRI Representations with Generative Adversarial Networks

Authors: Sujeet Lahane, Sapna Mishra, Akash Suryavanshi, Tapan Gandhi, Bharat Biswal

Abstract: Learning representations of visual images is a challenging task. It requires extracting abstract patterns from high-dimensional data without explicit supervision. This becomes pronounced with 3D MR imaging due to sparse features, higher dimensional inputs, and smaller datasets. Many state-of-the-art representation learning algorithms successfully rely on transformers and diffusion-based architectures. However, these methods struggle with smaller datasets. In effect, solution methods relax the problem by slicing 3D volumes and solving the problem for 2D medical imaging, resulting in a loss of spatial 3D information. We present R2RG, a representation learning method based on Convolutional Neural Networks (CNNs), with Generative Adversarial Networks (GANs) honing the reconstruction task. Our key insights lie in exploiting architectural biases in 3D CNNs, combined with data-efficient auto-encoding GANs and a composite loss function to enhance reconstruction fidelity and stabilize training. Our experiments on three standard datasets and a locally collected dataset show that R2RG not only achieves superior performance but also generalizes to heterogeneous and smaller datasets. Furthermore, representations learned with R2RG outperform those learned with contrastive-based representation learning methods on Parkinson's and Alzheimer's disease classification. Our work demonstrates that reconstruction-based representation learning is a promising direction for 3D medical imaging under limited data availability.

new Rethinking Cross-Sensor Domain Adaptation for Bridge Point Cloud Semantic Segmentation

Authors: Linghui Huang, Zipeng Wang, Shunjie Chen, Peng Guan, Jiandong Wang, Siqing Li, Luming Yang

Abstract: Segmentation models for bridge point clouds lose accuracy when trained on terrestrial laser scanning (TLS) data and applied to mobile laser scanning (MLS) data. This study re-examines the SemanticBridge TLS-to-MLS protocol to determine how sampling density, surface roughness, color, and acquisition viewpoint are expressed in the 8-cm sparse-voxel representation, and how adaptation gains depend on source-model convergence. Using one sparse-convolution U-Net, we combine raw-point statistics with controlled input perturbations and compare adaptive batch normalization, entropy minimization, Mean Teacher, and confidence-filtered self-training under 100- and 300-epoch schedules. TLS and MLS have similar nearest-neighbor spacing after preprocessing (12.08 and 12.02 mm), and a 4-mm perturbation of TLS points changes voxel occupancy by only 4.4%. Color distributions differ systematically, but zeroing color on identical voxels changes target mean intersection over union (mIoU) by only +0.02, and by +0.06 after the color augmentation is scale-corrected. The sensors also observe different portions of the bridge, yielding different class proportions. A 300-epoch source-only model obtains 57.80 mIoU on the paired-bridge protocol. Confidence-filtered self-training improves the 100-epoch baseline, but from a converged model it changes target mIoU by -0.97 (source-selected checkpoints) and -2.44 (final-50-epoch means). Repeated evaluation (standard deviation 0.27-0.64) and seed variation (1.88 across three seeds) are comparable to several method differences. Adaptation results on this benchmark therefore depend on representation scale, source-model convergence, and evaluation variability.

new V2VFusion: Text-Controlled Video-to-Video Diffusion for Degradation-Aware Video Fusion

Authors: Jiajun Chen, Han Xu, Yunfei Huang, Zhuoya Zhang, Guangcan Liu, Jiayi Ma

Abstract: Video fusion integrates complementary information in multiple source video streams into a single sequence for complete scene perception. Existing methods perform frame-wise fusion and lack unified spatio-temporal modeling across sequences, damaging temporal coherence across sequences. When dealing with degradations, they also ignore temporal dynamics and overload a single network with diverse degradations, leading to limited and imbalanced degradation suppression. To address these issues, we propose V2VFusion, the first unified video-to-video fusion framework that directly generates temporally coherent, high-quality fused videos from degraded multi-source inputs. By reformulating video fusion as a video-to-video conditional generation problem in latent diffusion space, our approach holistically models spatio-temporal dependencies across entire sequences and intrinsically ensures sequence-level consistency. On this basis, a text-guided degradation-conditioned ControlNet bridges high-level textual degradation descriptions with low-level visual priors, enabling interpretable and controllable guidance for structure-preserving fusion beyond purely data-driven conditioning. To handle heterogeneous and compound degradations, a state-modulated degradation-aware hierarchical mixture-of-experts module decouples expert selection into diffusion-state-aware filtering and degradation-aware routing. It prevents unreliable routing and fosters expert specialization to mitigate representation conflicts among diverse degradations. Experiments on infrared-visible, multi-exposure, and multi-focus video fusion tasks and complex degradations validate that V2VFusion surpasses existing methods in fusion quality and temporal stability. The code is available at https://github.com/ChenDoubleJ/V2VFusion.

URLs: https://github.com/ChenDoubleJ/V2VFusion.

new $R^2$: Recognizing and Reducing Redundant Visual Tool Use

Authors: Yilin Zhao, Jinyu Wang, Wenming Yang

Abstract: Recent studies have shown that visual tools can improve multimodal reasoning. However, the effects of tools vary substantially across samples, and benchmark accuracy alone does not reveal whether tool interactions are beneficial or redundant. We present $R^2$, a framework that recognizes and reduces redundant visual tool use in vision-language models. We first introduce a sample-level Category Analysis of Tool Utility (CAT), which categorizes samples according to whether a model can benefit from tools. This analysis reveals that benchmark scores may obscure a model's tool-use ability. Across seven benchmarks, samples benefiting from tools account for at most 12.44% of each benchmark, while the majority gain little from tool interaction or are even harmed by it. To assess individual calls, we propose the Visual Feedback Dependency (VFD) metric, which compares the likelihood of the same subsequent reasoning under actual and alternative visual feedback without an external evaluator or trajectory regeneration. Redundancy-Aware GRPO (RA-GRPO) combines this dependency signal with a category-conditioned base call cost to assign relative efficiency advantages among correct trajectories, supplementing outcome-based supervision. Across the seven benchmarks, RA-GRPO reduces the mean number of tool calls by 12.39% relative to Vanilla GRPO at comparable benchmark accuracy. Evaluated by tool-utility category, RA-GRPO improves category macro-average accuracy over Vanilla GRPO by 3.35 percentage points.

new Hindsight Forcing: Learning Better History from the Future in Autoregressive Video Diffusion

Authors: Haonan An, Yihang Tao, Yu Guo, Renlong Chen, Jiasheng Zhang, Yuguang Fang

Abstract: Few-step autoregressive video diffusion enables real-time streaming generation, with each generated chunk serving as historical context for subsequent generation. To avoid the memory explosion of backpropagation through time, previous self-rollout methods typically truncate cross-chunk gradients or detach historical latents. As a result, earlier denoising computations receive no downstream feedback through the generated history on how to synthesize frames that facilitate subsequent generation, leading to visual drift and structural collapse as rollouts extend. To address this limitation, we propose Hindsight Forcing, which allows historical chunks to learn directly from future outcomes by decoupling forward generation from backward credit assignment via a latent straight-through bridge. In the forward pass, each chunk completes a full multi-step rollout to update the KV cache. In the backward pass, the bridge routes gradients from future losses back to a single selected denoising step per historical chunk, training both the context writer and historical denoiser on shared parameters without retaining the full trajectory graph. Remarkably, Hindsight Forcing requires no additional model parameters, architecture modifications, or separate trajectory replay. Extensive experiments on VBench demonstrate that Hindsight Forcing outperforms existing baselines in overall visual quality and dynamic motion, while ablation studies validate the benefit of supervising historical denoising. Code is available at https://github.com/haonanAN309/Hindsight-Forcing.

URLs: https://github.com/haonanAN309/Hindsight-Forcing.

new PIU-CR: Physics-Informed Deep Unfolding with SAR-Optical Image Fusion for Cloud Removal

Authors: Gengque Fan, Han Xu, Junxi Li, Hao Zhang, Guangcan Liu, Jiayi Ma

Abstract: Multi-modal cloud removal exploits complementary optical and synthetic aperture radar (SAR) images to restore cloud-degraded remote sensing images. Most existing methods rely on black-box, data-driven architectures with limited interpretability of cross-modal interactions. Although some attempts introduce physical priors into networks, their oversimplified formulations fail to faithfully characterize the underlying physical imaging process and the networks remain loosely coupled with optimization variables. To address these issues, we propose a physics-informed deep unfolding network for cloud removal via optical-SAR image fusion, termed as PIU-CR. We first formulate cloud removal as a physics-informed optimization problem. Specifically, we fundamentally ground the optimization in the physical imaging mechanism of cloud degradation by embedding the atmospheric scattering model into the data fidelity term. Then, the regularization terms establish an explicit cross-modal interaction mechanism, overcoming the single-modal information scarcity and the obscure, uninterpretable interactions in black-box models. They are dedicated to SAR-guided structure and spectral regularization, jointly introducing cross-modal structural feature alignment and coordinated spectral consistency constraints. The resulting optimization problem is then unfolded into a multi-stage neural network with dedicated structure and spectral branches. Each stage corresponds to an explicit optimization iteration step, enabling interpretable and effective reconstruction process. Experiments demonstrate that the proposed method outperforms state-of-the-art methods. The code is publicly available at https://github.com/Fgq188/PIU_CR.

URLs: https://github.com/Fgq188/PIU_CR.

new UDA-Restoration: Bridging Unsupervised Domain Adaptation and Multi-Degradation Denoising for Enhanced AV Semantic Segmentation

Authors: Zainab Bibi, Mohsin Kamal

Abstract: Autonomous vehicles rely heavily on vision-based perception systems for safe navigation and decision-making. Adverse weather conditions, sensor noise, moving artifacts and illumination changes, however, often degrade the image quality and hence reduce perception performance. This paper introduces a diffusion-guided unsupervised domain adaptation algorithm for autonomous driving environments, namely UDA-Restoration, which is designed to enhance and denoise images. The proposed method follows the paradigm of Noise-Space Domain Adaptation (Noise-DA), which is based on multi-source degradation learning and diffusion-guided adaptation that spans across synthetic and real-world degradations. The framework has been extensively tested and shown to restore image quality and to be robust to unseen degradation scenarios, such as snow, haze, sensor distortions, and more. The quantitative results indicate that Noise-DA outperforms the evaluated self-supervised and unsupervised baselines while matching the best supervised restoration performance. In addition, semantic segmentation experiments show that the performance of downstream perception tasks is directly improved by better image restoration. The outcomes show the promise of unsupervised domain adaptation based on diffusion as a powerful and scalable method for image restoration tasks in autonomous driving systems under various environments.

new EgoSurgHands: An Egocentric 3D Hand Pose Dataset & Benchmark for Open Surgery Training

Authors: Ankit Pal, Guillaume Kugener, Sofia Volpi, Joshua Schwartz, Alex Idarraga, Pranav Rajpurkar, Gabriel A. Brat

Abstract: Surgical skill assessment, AR guidance, and robot learning from demonstration all benefit from 3D tracking of the surgeon's hands, yet no surgical dataset offers real egocentric articulated 3D hand pose. We introduce EgoSurgHands, a benchmark for real-captured egocentric hand pose estimation in open surgery training on synthetic pads. Captured with multi-sensor glasses, it contains 41,909 training hand-instance rows across 55 procedure recordings and 9,476 human-validated evaluation rows across 13 held-out recordings, spanning 13 tasks, 3 glove colors, and 4 trainees from first-year medical student to third-year resident. Each frame carries a multi-sensor stack: intrinsics, hand landmarks with per-hand confidence, timestamps, and eye gaze, plus SLAM extrinsics where SLAM succeeded. Evaluation labels combine sensor-fused pseudo-ground truth, human cross-review of every frame, surgeon adjudication of flagged cases, and an independent multi-view triangulated reference. Alongside the within-corpus, procedure-disjoint split, a leave-one-wearer-out protocol tested unseen trainees. Across eight methods evaluated zero-shot, from MANO-based reconstructors to the two-hand IntagHand and the keypoint-only MediaPipe Hands, egocentric pretraining was associated with stronger transfer on every held-out wearer. WildHands and JointTransformer had 18\% and 40\% lower PA-MPJPE, respectively, than the non-egocentric HaMeR. JointTransformer was the best performing model with an error of 14.95 mm. A substantial surgical domain gap remains, and the widely deployed MediaPipe Hands detected a hand in only 24.1\% of evaluation rows. As a baseline, a 58K-parameter residual corrector (JRN) on frozen backbones reached 10.96-14.85 mm PA-MPJPE on held-out wearers for the two ego-pretrained backbones. We release the dataset and benchmark code at https://egosurghands.github.io

URLs: https://egosurghands.github.io

new One state, many questions: Opsis gives a frontier model typed eyes for pathology

Authors: Zhixuan Ge, Wei Qiu

Abstract: A synoptic pathology report is a checklist of questions with fixed answer sets, many composed from others. Pathology foundation models answer them accurately with one classifier trained with labels for each, and frontier language models, which compose freely, cannot fit a slide of fourteen thousand tiles in their context. On 189 breast report questions, frontier models that read a written description of the slide or look at a thumbnail and tiles of it answer at 0.50 to 0.66, below the majority class at 0.67. A description is written before the question is known, and a look covers a handful of tiles. We present Opsis, which lets the model ask instead: a typed question interface on a frozen pathology encoder. A slide is encoded once, a question names an operator, arguments and candidates given at runtime, and the answer is a probability over the candidates. We build it as a 10 M parameter decoder on frozen Virchow2 tokens (Opsis-S) and inside Qwen3-VL-8B (Opsis-V). Asking Opsis-S, four frontier models answer the breast questions at 0.78 to 0.79, and on 3,797 pan cancer questions asking is 8.6 points above reading. On 995 composite questions that Opsis-S was never trained on, a frontier model composing its answers reaches 0.76, against 0.69 when it looks at the slide and 0.78 for ABMIL trained on each composite. On a pan cancer question set over 10,712 TCGA slides, one Opsis-S decoder matches linear probes trained per question on TITAN and PRISM2 embeddings (average accuracy 0.69 against 0.70 and 0.68). Opsis-S also separates CAMELYON16 metastases without training on that cohort (AUROC 0.86) and localises necrosis from report labels alone (tile AUROC 0.93).

new RemoteEvolver: Expanding Earth Observation Capabilities through Agent Harness Evolution

Authors: Shengxiang Xu, Liang Yao, Shuyi Chen, Yishu Wang, Shimin Di, Fan Liu, Bishun Yao, Hanghui Guo, Xinyu Liu, Chengye Liang, Yuyu Luo, Min-Ling Zhang

Abstract: We are interested in developing a continually self-improving remote sensing agentic system. It should identify and address its capability gaps, retaining the resulting improvements to enhance performance on subsequent tasks. However, existing systems typically rely on fixed toolsets and confine their experience accumulation to re-orchestrating predefined capabilities, constraining both capability expansion and the transfer of improvements across tasks. As the infrastructure for agentic systems, the harness manages tools and workflow knowledge as editable, persistent resources outside the base model. These resources can therefore be updated and reused across tasks without additional post-training. Based on these observations, we propose RemoteEvolver, an external harness for remote sensing agents that drives tool and workflow evolution through execution feedback. With the base model parameters fixed, RemoteEvolver acquires missing specialized tools on demand and distills verified successful execution experience from individual tasks into agent skills that can be reused across tasks. The acquired tools and accumulated skills jointly support planning and execution on subsequent tasks. Experiments on Earth-Bench and OpenEarthAgent-Bench show that RemoteEvolver achieves average accuracy gains of 8.18 and 23.86 percentage points over its initial state when tools are fully available and partially missing. Under the same settings, it also outperforms the state-of-the-art methods by an average of 6.77 and 17.10 percentage points, respectively.

new Analyzing Multi-Layer Visual Representations of Large Vision-Language Models for Anomaly Detection

Authors: Satoshi Hashimoto, Hitoshi Nishimura, Mori Kurokawa

Abstract: In this paper, we analyze multi-layer visual representations in large vision-language models (LVLMs) and examine the effectiveness of layer-wise features and multi-layer fusion for anomaly detection. Representative LVLMs connect visual encoder features to a language model through a projector. In this design, deep visual encoder features carry high-level semantics and alignment with text representations, whereas shallow and intermediate layers retain information relevant to vision-centric tasks, such as local appearance and fine-grained shape. It is therefore important to choose layer representations and fusion methods according to the nature of the downstream task. Focusing on anomaly detection, we compare layer-wise features and multi-layer fusion across multiple supervision levels and a variety of classifiers, and examine from several perspectives how multi-layer visual representations affect downstream performance. By analyzing the resulting performance differences, we further discuss the contribution of each layer representation to anomaly detection and provide empirical insights into representation selection for vision-centric tasks.

new Crafting the Difference, Grounding the Evidence: Discriminative Concept Learning for Zero-Shot Skeleton Action Recognition

Authors: Siyu Zhu, Feng Ding, Zixiao Jin, Kaizhi Li, Xin Dong, Haisheng Fu, Yuchen Zou, Guohe Zhang, Jie Liang

Abstract: LLM-generated descriptions enrich zero-shot skeleton-based action recognition but repeat cues shared by confusable actions. Their contextual semantics also lack explicit links to skeleton features. To address these challenges, we propose CRAFT, which couples Concept Refinement and Feature-grounded Tokens. Hard-negative-conditioned generation and contrastive scoring identify concepts that distinguish each action from its closest rivals. Unique-QDPP selects complementary descriptions within each semantic slot and prevents their reuse across slots. This bank supervises learnable concept tokens that read motion and context cues from each skeleton. On fixed NTU-60 and NTU-120 splits, CRAFT improves five baselines spanning four recognition paradigms. ZSL accuracy and GZSL harmonic mean increase on every baseline-split pair, by 5.4 and 5.9 percentage points on average. Random-split experiments extend these gains to three datasets, including PKU-MMD. Code will be released after acceptance.

new TrajRealm: A Large-Scale Point Tracking Dataset with Diverse Dynamics

Authors: Ni Yang, Weiguang Zhao, Jiafei Cao, Yankai Jiang, Haoyu Guo, Tao Yang, Chunhua Shen, Junting Dong

Abstract: General-purpose point tracking requires large-scale training data with diverse dynamics, yet existing datasets struggle to combine rich dynamic content, accurate scene geometry, and sufficient scale. We introduce TrajRealm, the first public synthetic point-tracking dataset to reach the ten-million-frame scale, constructed from games and embodied simulations. TrajRealm uses the rich environments and motion systems of modern games to capture diverse targets, activities, and interactions across diverse worlds, while embodied simulations add robot manipulation and indoor navigation data. The dataset provides RGB observations, depth, camera parameters, and persistent 2D and 3D point trajectories. These annotations provide precise, geometrically consistent supervision across foreground objects and static environmental surfaces. We also build TrajRealm-Bench with eight subcategories under Interaction and Locomotion to evaluate tracking across diverse scenes. Benchmark evaluations reveal the strengths and limitations of existing trackers in handling complex motion and maintaining long-term point correspondences. Training on TrajRealm improves the performance of existing tracking methods on synthetic and real-world benchmarks.

new How Does SAM-3 Adapt? Capacity, Update Geometry, and Domain Transfer

Authors: Allan Kazakov, Abdurrahman Javat, Duygu Cakir

Abstract: Promptable segmentation foundation models are increasingly used in specialized domains, but it remains unclear how SAM-3 should be fine-tuned when adapting it beyond its original training distribution. We study this question by comparing five adaptation families --LoRA, HiRA, KRAdapter, QuanTA, and Full Fine-Tuning-- across five segmentation datasets spanning medical imaging, remote sensing, and dense material segmentation. Across these datasets, compact parameter-efficient fine-tuning methods often match or approach Full Fine-Tuning in DSC and IoU, suggesting that strong in-domain adaptation does not always require updating the full model. However, this performance cannot be explained by nominal adapter rank or trainable parameter count alone. To investigate why different methods perform similarly, we analyze their update spectra and their behavior under LoveDA Urban/Rural transfer. Our SVD analysis shows that Full Fine-Tuning updates are spectrally compressible. However, successful PEFT methods do not simply recover the same update directions; instead, they often concentrate changes in different components and subspaces. The domain-transfer study further shows that methods with similar in-domain performance can respond differently under distribution shift. Together, these results suggest that SAM-3 adaptation is often capacity-saturated in practice, while competitive fine-tuning arises through method-specific update paths rather than a single low-rank approximation to Full Fine-Tuning.

new Real-Anchored Complementary Evidence for Generalizable AI-Generated Image Detection

Authors: Wenpeng Mu, Tanfeng Sun, Zhenyu Shao, Qiang Xu, Xinghao Jiang

Abstract: Evolving generative models challenge detector generalization, as methods trained on generated examples may rely on generator-specific cues. By contrast, authentic photographs share regularities rooted in physical image formation and acquisition, providing a common reference beyond individual generators. Building on these regularities, we propose Real-Anchored Complementary Evidence (RACE), which learns complementary global and local detection evidence using only authentic training images. First, the global pathway combines frozen and learned representations to assess proximity to authentic references. Next, it incorporates responses to controlled perturbations, complementing static compatibility with representation sensitivity. The local pathway then reconstructs complete trace descriptors, using intermediate features and signed perturbation responses as context while restricting detection evidence to static terminal-residual reconstruction errors. Finally, authentic-image statistics align the global and local scores for fusion, and an independent authentic set calibrates a decision threshold held fixed across test generators. Across six benchmarks, RACE achieves the highest average AUROC of 96.59% among the compared methods, reaching 97.18% on five out-of-domain benchmarks.
